ParlaMint-ES_2023-01-24-CD230124.u108	Thank you very much, Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen, today is eleven months since Putin invaded Ukraine. On 24 February 2022 it will be remembered singularly because it is the day on which the war returned to Europe, eleven months in which Ukraine has resisted fighting for its freedom and independence. The fighting, of course, is taking place there, on Ukrainian soil, but the consequences of the Russian aggression go much further, impact head-on in the European Union - let us remember that four of its Member States share a border with Ukraine - and strike in full in many other regions of the world either affected by the energy crisis or faced with a food crisis that can further exacerbate famines in many countries of the African continent already punished by the effects of climate change. We are faced, ladies and gentlemen - we have said this from this rostrum repeatedly over these eleven months - with an unscrupulous autocrat who knows no limits, who is willing to use everything regardless of the price to be paid, provided, of course, that it is others who pay it. Concepts that we gave for good, for sure, are again in question: an international order based on rules, the inviolability of borders, respect for a country's decision to choose freely over its future without interference or coercion. We believed that multilateralism had been enshrined in order to resolve conflicts, to guarantee peace in Europe; we trusted in the expansion of democracies as the best guarantee for the prosperity of our nations. And in that context, ladies and gentlemen, since 24 February 2022, Spain, together with the rest of the European Union, has been and will be on the side of Ukraine, not only with the sending of military material, but also with humanitarian aid, imposing economic sanctions on the Russian oligarchs and hosting refugees as long as necessary. Spain - you know - is one of the most geographically remote countries in Europe from Ukraine, but we are, together with Italy, the fourth country with the most refugees welcomed, only behind Poland, Germany and the Czech Republic. In total there are 164 000 Ukrainians and Ukrainians who have received temporary protection and reside in our country with full rights. Similarly, we have allocated a figure never seen in humanitarian aid to a single country, EUR 38 million, for humanitarian aid throughout the conflict zone. In short, ladies and gentlemen, Spain has supported and will continue to support, as it could not otherwise be, together with its European allies, Ukraine as long as the occupation lasts, also logically, in its task of present and future reconstruction. As I said to you, ladies and gentlemen, many certainties seem to be in question today, and not just in the area of European security and therefore global. The year we left behind, 2022, has meant that inflation will hit record figures in the past thirty-five years; the energy crisis clearly distresses the whole of Europe as it has not since the end of the last century, in the 1970s, and the rise in interest rates, a product of tightening monetary policies around the world, and also in Europe, hits Spanish families with a variable mortgage. Today, in this context, ladies and gentlemen, within the framework of these European Councils of October and December, I intend to speak to you, as I said, of the present and above all of the future, and for this reason I think it is essential not to forget some of the lessons of the past, because it is usually said, rightly, that history does not repeat itself, but that it rhymes, and today that rhyme warns us - we are seeing it in other geographical latitudes - against reactionary movements that propose the prescriptions for the closure of borders and also of autocracies, and, together with this, the prophets of Revelation reappear, those who warn so much of profound crises end up confusing their desires with reality; they doubt our economy, they damage the image of our country and do not hesitate to do so even outside our country if it suits their purposes. That is why, ladies and gentlemen, I believe it is important both to make the diagnosis right and not to hide from people the risks and challenges we face, both in Europe and in Spain. I believe that this is an exercise in which we have been engaged since the first day of the conflict on 24 February in the Executive. We have not hidden the difficulties ahead, we have never sweetened reality, we have not painted idyllic scenarios either because the risks are there, they continue to exist and they continue to have a real impact on the economy of our homes and also on the economy as a whole. I therefore believe, ladies and gentlemen, that the essential thing is to make the right move, to turn to what matters, and what is important is to protect the social majority of our country, to move forward in what is often invoked, which is useful politics, and useful politics is nothing more than to try to solve the problems of people, the real problems of people; of the others we are going to have this afternoon, I imagine, rather than to talk throughout the replicas and duplicates with some parliamentary opposition groups. But I think it is important to talk about the real problems of the people, of the workers who take the closeness to go to work on a daily basis, of the families who devote much of their payroll to the payment of mortgage or rent, of the self-employed, of the small and medium-sized enterprises that have less capacity to fit the rise in light prices or problems in the supply chains, of the households that have many difficulties in being able to reach the end of the month because the wage does not give for more. In any case, ladies and gentlemen, the year 2022, as I said before, with an inflation rate of 8.4%, and this general figure for the economy as a whole has different impacts, its consequences are not the same for all. For some family with incomes above €100,000 per year, this 8.4% is a setback; for a family with incomes below €30,000 per year, this 8.4% has dramatic effects. In the home of the 100,000 euros in income, adjustments will obviously have to be made; in the home where one earns three times less, those 200 euros of loss per month are the difference between living and surviving. Ladies and gentlemen, I have heard this on many occasions, we, as the executive, govern for all citizens, but it is clear to this government that priority must be given to it: we govern for this social majority, which is so often relegated during the worst years of the financial crisis. I said it since the first day of the war, and it is that, as we also did during the pandemic, we are going to put all the resources of the state at the service of the majority and not the other way around, much less at the service of a powerful minority, as long as the one who can save the majority of the people is condemned. [[Applauds]] This time, I insist, as also happened in our response to COVID-19, the social response will be imposed and that social response will be imposed in the face of the failed neoliberal response of the financial crisis. This time there will be increases in pensions according to the CPI and not its freezing, as happened in the past, while they were rescued - by the way, with public money - broken banks. This time there will be increases in the inter-professional minimum wage and not its freezing, as happened in the past, while the government leaders of the time paid extra in A and B. This time there will be tax cuts to the middle class and to the working class, and not indiscriminate tax increases to that middle class and worker while tax amnesties were passed by the government of the time for large fraudsters. [[Applauds]] This time, ladies and gentlemen, there will be fiscal justice because we intend, and are also committed to, to rebuild that social cohesion broken by the failed neoliberal response to the financial crisis. By the way, ladies and gentlemen, having learned the data of the European Banking Authority, in which Spanish banking is placed as the place where the bulk of managers with higher and better salaries throughout Europe are concentrated, I think it is absolutely common sense to ask them to make a greater effort with the creation of the tax on large financial institutions. [[Applauds]] In any case, as we have always said, first, with the pandemic and, now, with this war, to govern is to choose, always is, always is, and the Government chooses to protect people on foot, as we did, I insist once again, during the pandemic, and we do so not only because we are a progressive coalition government committed to social justice, but because I believe that, in addition, the data corroborate, certify, that this is the most economically effective response. The data indicate this. Ten years ago, if we take a look at the media for a month in January 2013, we will see how it was said, it was reported in those media that the unemployment rate in our country was 26%; Spain suffered unemployment that reached 6 million unemployed in Spain. We lost a million working days as a result of labour conflict. The youth unemployment rate was at an absolute stratospheric limit of 57%, and the working population fell due to the emigration, above all massive, of many young people seeking the opportunities that their country denied them. The neoliberal response of the then government was to impose many counter-reforms without social agreement, one of them, particularly injuring, was the counter-reform of work that accelerated, did not brake, but accelerated, the mass dismissal of workers, devalued workers' wages and also precarious their working conditions. Today, in Europe, ladies and gentlemen, few already defend that legacy of ashes - there are nostalgic, logically, throughout Europe, also in Spain - about this neoliberal response to the financial crisis, but they are not a majority. In any case, to the citizens who are following this debate, I will give you two eloquent facts that I believe certify why I am defending this social response, not only for justice but also for economic efficiency, as we have done during the pandemic to war, in contrast to the financial crisis. With the neoliberal response then, it took eight years, ladies and gentlemen, eight years!, to recover the previous levels of gross domestic product before the financial crisis, and it took ten years to recover the levels of employment before the financial crisis. With the social response that we have been giving over these complex three years, with all the difficulties and also with the mistakes that we have been able to make, in two years we have recovered the pre-pandemic employment level, we will soon recover the previous levels of GDP, and inequality, ladies and gentlemen, has not increased, as it did during the financial crisis. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, after the beginning of the war, I explained from this very rostrum what the answers would be, the plan to respond to the economic and social consequences of the Putin war in Ukraine. I would also like to remind you that, in two areas - the national and the European, because there are many things that depend on Europe - the different answers that we have to give at national level, and also to propose to our European partners that they materialise. At the national level, the response plan has been mobilised to date by 45 billion euros, 45 billion euros!, and the results of this gigantic effort are in sight. We have been able to end 2022 with an economic growth rate that is going to be above 5% of gross domestic product, with the lowest inflation in Europe; we have reduced inflation by five months 5 points, with a record of social security membership of the entire historical series and the lowest unemployment in the last fifteen years. You mean this, ladies and gentlemen, we have little work left to do? On the contrary, we have a lot of work to do, we do not settle, but I think the results are there and what they do is to certify the success or effectiveness of our responses and encourage us to follow this path. [[Applauds]] At European level, ladies and gentlemen, we have promoted measures that would be unthinkable before the war. To think that Europe was going to walk by the centralized purchase of gas from third countries was a chimera before the war began on 24 February; the impulse to renewables, where Spain is an example of inspiration for many, is something already assumed, even by those countries still doubtful to be committed to that green transition; the destination of European funds to the green transition, but also to digital transformation, and the intervention in the price of gas, electricity, what has been called the Iberian solution, or the impulse to energy interconnections, both electric and hydrogen, with Barcelona as the main European player, I think are some, probably the main, measures that Spain has been leading in Brussels. I say all this, ladies and gentlemen, because I believe that this is also a big difference from what happened in the financial crisis because it is not about having language or not, it is about fighting in Brussels and winning them for the benefit of our country, but also of Europe, because today Spain exports solutions to the energy and price crisis, unlike what happened ten years ago under other governments, with which, if we import something, they were rescues to the financial sector and cuts to our welfare state. [[Applauds]] On all this, ladies and gentlemen, we have spoken at the European Councils in October and December, and I would also like to do so, recalling a fact which caught my attention when I read it the other day, and which is the latest Eurobarometer, recently published, where it was said that 81% of our compatriots feel like European citizens, is 10 points above the European average, ladies and gentlemen, 10 points above the European average. This figure I believe shows many things, but it certainly shows a sense of belonging of Spain to Europe, of its citizens with this common project and with the values and principles it represents, and it also shows confidence in the European project, confidence in order to protect ourselves from threats, which we can only obviously face together. One of these threats is that related to a key concept that we are going to develop, increasingly common in the public debate, which is that of open strategic autonomy, in short, how we gain European sovereignty in the face of the threats ahead. Not being autonomous makes us vulnerable, and I think we have a lot of experience over the past three years to be able to support this reflection; we learned it in the hardest part of the pandemic, when Europe suddenly found itself without enough masks and breathers to protect life from the emergence of a virus like the COVID-19. If it was the pandemic before, today is the energy because of the war, the blackmail of Putin with gas and oil, but tomorrow can be other areas, such as that of new technologies or data. In short, whatever that threat may be, I believe it is our duty to plan the response and gain that sovereignty in European terms. This is the first lesson I would like to share with all of you, and I believe that Europe cannot and should not hesitate. It is essential to move forward in this open strategic autonomy, with the certainty, moreover, that the prosperity of our societies cannot depend on the whims of any autocracy. Strategic autonomy, to gain European sovereignty, means having the capacity to decide in key sectors for our economic development and also for the prosperity of our societies, whether in the health field, as I have mentioned before, in defence, in technology or in industry, and we will not be able to reach it knowing, for example, that - I give you this data - less than 10% of the microchips that are manufactured in the world leave Europe, or that only three countries concentrate, nothing more and nothing less, than 90% of the production of lithium, which is essential for the manufacture of electric car batteries or energy storage, or that Europe is losing the career of deploying 5G networks to global powers with which we compete, such as China or the United States. For that reason, ladies and gentlemen, I think it is a good thing that, together with the development of this idea of social Europe, strategic autonomy, European sovereignty is one of the main priorities of the Spanish Presidency of the European Union in the second half of this year. It is a challenge in which we will work to reduce vulnerabilities and strengthen our autonomy in critical sectors, in strategic sectors. The second lesson is offered by recent history. As I said before, a decade ago a wrong response, a neoliberal response to a financial crisis, seriously affected the foundations of the European project; a response that was insolidary and that led to an unjust, slow recovery and, thus, the citizen's failure to trust the European project. For that reason, I believe that advocating and defending a just response, with all the difficulties that this crisis caused by the war in Ukraine has, not only serves to protect the social majority of our country and our continent, it is also an investment that re-arms that qualitative concept, which is the confidence and legitimacy of the European project so necessary or more necessary than ever. On that path, ladies and gentlemen, Spain is today a protagonist in the most relevant issues, and the best example of this is in the energy field. At the last two meetings of the European Council in October and December, two issues dominated the debates: firstly, energy and, secondly, the response we have given from Europe to the law on inflation reduction adopted by the Biden Administration in the United States. I'll start with the first thing, the energy. In 2022, as I said before, steps have been taken in a direction in which Spain has been working for a long time. You have heard us say this to the various responsible, both the ministers and myself. In particular, the European Councils in October and December adopted guidelines in a number of areas, later translated into legislative agreements at meetings of our Energy Ministers at the European Council. For example, on 15 December the Council of the Union of Energy Ministers adopted, after intensive discussions, a package of three very important energy regulations. The first, the so-called market correction mechanism; for us to understand, in colloquial terms, involves the recognition of the intervention of a market that does not work by the public administration, in this case, of the regulation, that is, a ceiling at the price of import gas to guarantee the supply of gas at an affordable price; obviously, with different conditions, but this is an open door through which Spain has been advocating for a very long time, even before the war began. And we do this by thinking, logically, of industry, of businesses and of families in Europe and our country. Along with this market correction mechanism or gas price cap, the solidarity strengthening regulation was adopted, which includes, as I said earlier, joint purchases, centralised purchases of gas and the revision of the benchmarks that Europe uses for gas price determination. Thirdly, the regulation entailing the further acceleration of the deployment of renewable energies. Ladies and gentlemen, in the drafting, negotiation and adoption of these three important regulations, the influence of Spain has been decisive, to say the least. To a large extent these finally agreed texts respond to what we have been demanding from Spain since the beginning of the energy crisis. In addition to these three important regulations, the European debate on the reform of the European electricity market intensified in the last quarter of 2022, something which Spain began to defend as early as September 2021, that is, many months before the start of the war, and which is finally taking place in Europe with the commitment of the European Executive, of the European Commission, to present a proposal this year. As you know, in this context Spain has already submitted a proposal - we were the first country to do so - which insists on a fundamental question, and it is that of disconnecting the evolution of the price of renewable energies from the evolution of the price of gas. Again, I do not think it is an exaggeration to say that the Spanish proposal has received great support from the Member States and that it has become the basis for all the debates on this important, I would say, decisive issue. [[Applauds]] Along with this, I would also like to focus on the competitiveness and re-industrialisation of Europe, subjects, of course, connected with the debate on energy prices. As you know, the US Administration's approval of the so-called "Inflation Reduction Act" implies that the world's first economy will allocate nearly $370,000 million to subsidize investments in strategic industrial sectors linked to the green transition. We are talking about hydrogen projects, batteries, electric cars and renewable energies, among many others. Logically, making subsidies conditional on the manufacture on US territory of the entire value chain of these products, what it does is to put at risk the relocation and the divestment of investments from the European Union to the United States. In this respect, I would like to say to the House what is Spain's position on the basis of three considerations that I would like to make before you. First of all, it is good news that a government – in this case, a democratic government – does quite the opposite of what Trump did when he ruled the United States, which went out of the Paris Accords. It is therefore good news that the U.S. government is pushing through these measures its commitment to combating climate change, measures that, logically, are making progress towards an accelerated decarbonisation of its economy, but this goal cannot be achieved at the cost of deindustrializing Europe and many other emerging economies on the planet. Secondly, and therefore, what we are doing is working towards an agreement in the current negotiations in order to exclude European products from the most protectionist clauses of this American law. The United States and the European Union are partners, we are global allies, especially in this very complex context arising from the Putin war in Ukraine, we share democratic values and principles, so it seems reasonable that we should go hand in hand on this very important issue for our economies. Finally, I believe that we too must do our duty, that Europe must react to protect its interests, and this is what we agreed at the last European Council in December, to ask the Commission to present a coordinated response plan to improve European industrial resilience, to improve our competitiveness on a global scale and to preserve, at the same time, the integrity of one of the main achievements of the European project, which is the single market. This plan, which will be presented in the coming days by the European Commission, must have measures in the short, medium and long term of regulation, financing and reform of the state aid framework to make it more agile and adapted to the current context, and to do so, in addition, including the acceleration of recovery, transformation and resilience plans, in short, of European funds, which has been defending Spain since we started this debate. I would certainly say to your ladies and gentlemen that this is a matter of the utmost importance, because crucial investments are at stake for the whole of Europe and, above all, what is at stake, both in the present and in the future, is the capacity of the Union and its industrial fabric in an international geopolitical context marked by the derivative protectionist powers such as the United States and also China and India. In this very uncertain context, ladies and gentlemen, we have done important things not only by offering solvency, but also solidarity, for example, in all the aftermath of the energy crisis: we have reduced the demand for natural gas by 23% since August, compared to the average consumption of the last five years, and also by more than 7% in the case of electricity demand. The easy thing, ladies and gentlemen, would have been to throw away the towel when our proposals were received with a certain scepticism not only in Brussels, but also here by those who are always where they are, in the no, but we never fell down, and thanks to that today we no longer row alone, today we are accompanied by many governments with which we are moving in a good direction, which is to protect the social majority of our country. In any case, some resistances were predictable, it is logical, for example, those of the big energy corporations when we said that the war in Ukraine could not be an alibi for obtaining extraordinary benefits in sectors that have benefited precisely from this crisis - in every crisis there are always sectors that benefit from the suffering of the majority -; they are logical resistances because, after all, we are talking about their results account. Other resistances, on the other hand, were more inexplicable, such as those of those who manoeuvre so that such fiscal justice measures would not prosper in Brussels. The same happened later with what some called the Iberian thymus. Those same were the ones who spoke of the Iberian thymus, to which everyone now calls the Iberian solution, a measure that has already saved, in less than a year, more than 45 billion euros to Spanish households. We are talking about an average of 150 euros in each household. Thanks to this Iberian solution, ladies and gentlemen, the price of the wholesale market, since it entered into force, has been 195 euros the megawatt, that is, 20% less than what would have been recorded in the absence of this mechanism in Spain and 53% less than in Germany, 58% less than in France and 63% less than the price in Italy. Finally, with this data, it is logical that other countries that do defend the general interest of their societies want for them what Spain and Portugal have fought with such determination. [[Applauds]] Because, ladies and gentlemen, this is what it is about; it is about fighting, not lowering your head, and this is the decisive factor, because Spain today dares to wage those battles in Brussels and has learned to win them. Others accepted submissive a doctrine charged with moral reproach against our country and southern Europe, importing bank bailouts, while Spain today exports solutions such as the Iberian solution. Ladies and gentlemen, as I recalled at the beginning of my speech, eleven months of courageous resistance by the Ukrainian people to Putin are being fulfilled today. In the last Councils, the Union has once again shown unity, firmness and determination in this purpose, in all areas, military, political, economic and humanitarian. Europe's commitment to Ukraine is Europe's commitment also to democracy, to the rules-based international order and, ultimately, to peace and not to the law of the strongest. Ukraine is resisting not only the invader, ladies and gentlemen, but also resisting a reactionary discourse that questions the values and foundations of the European project. It is a speech that some people replicate in other latitudes, as we have seen in Brazil, a little more in the United States and, also, in Spain, and which means nothing more and nothing less than going back to a time we thought had passed, a time in black and white. To all of them, ladies and gentlemen, I say, in the seat of national sovereignty, that they will never succeed, that the rights they have won are not questioned, but are defended, respected and exercised. And if others, out of fear or post-electoral calculations, tremble their legs to defend them, I tell them that the government will never shake its hand, that we will not give in for a minute. [[Applauds]] Three years ago, in my investiture speech, I formalized before you the pillars of the Government's proposal for a progressive coalition for these four years of legislature. We did it before, as you will recall, a few weeks before we had to approve the state of alarm in order to deal with an unknown virus. There were four axes of action: the first, that of digital transformation; the second, that of ecological transition; the third, that of cohesion, both social and territorial, and, the fourth, that of coexistence. Certainly, in 2018, ladies and gentlemen, after the motion of censure, we did not receive a peaceful Spain; we did not receive it. Spain suffered a political crisis as a result of the continuing scandals of corruption that plagued those who ruled at the time and, together with it, a territorial crisis, which led Spain to an unprecedented precipice in the history of democracy, to break our territorial integrity and also its constitutional order, and all this with the backdrop of a serious social crisis, of a deep inequality, whose maximum exponent continues to be child poverty. [[protests]] Today, ladies and gentlemen, there is no doubt that, if we had opted for the deployment of renewable energies and not for a sun tax, Spain would be better prepared to cope with the energy crisis. [[Applauds]] Today, ladies and gentlemen, there is no doubt, except for some recalcitrants, that later we will have the opportunity to hear again - I was going to say his reflections - that Catalonia and, therefore, Spain is better today than in 2017. Today, ladies and gentlemen, there is no doubt that Spain would have been better prepared for the COVID if it had had the 94 000 health professionals who had joined the system during these four years of progressive coalition government, compared to the 3000 health professionals who had been cut during conservative governments. [[Applauds]] All these transformations, pending for years - the reasons each one will be able to find them wherever he thinks - we have addressed them during this time so complex, so difficult. We do not choose crises, but we do choose how to deal with that crisis, and we do so by protecting those below, demanding greater responsibility, if I may say so, those above and never forget, never, ever, that, in the face of the multiple crises that we are suffering right now in Spain, in Europe and in the world, we must also reconcile these immediate responses with the great transformations to ensure the present and future prosperity of Spain. What am I talking about? Today I am referring to that in Spain we are betting on clean energy as the best formula to guarantee not only energy sovereignty and cope with Putin's energy blackmail, but also to respond to that greater challenge that humanity has ahead, which is the climate emergency. Today there is a government that promotes the digitization of its companies, the digital training of its workers, which recognizes labour rights to those workers on digital platforms. Today there is a government that is committed to public health, that launches the largest public supply of specialized health training, as happened last weekend, in particular 171 new places, to strengthen, among others, primary care, while before there was an executive that opened the doors to the privatization of health care and that established co-sponsors, today happily repealed as a result of the commitment of the parliamentary majority in this House, as well as the government. [[Applauds]] That same commitment, ladies and gentlemen, is the one we have been deploying and the one that explains the measures to contain the inflation caused by the war in Ukraine. We face it from two objectives, which I would like to share with all of you. The first of these is the obvious one: to alleviate the impact, to the extent of our possibilities, of rising prices in households, businesses and industries. Secondly, to progressively reduce these levels of inflation. A few minutes ago, and I am grateful to the House, this Parliament adopted the sixth package of measures in response to the war, with which we are acting on four fronts, which I would like to share with all of you. First of all, what we are doing is supporting the people, the families of our country. Thousands of homes suffer to fill the refrigerator every week, to buy clothes, to pay bills. I am aware that having reduced the annual rate by 5 points in five months is not enough to improve the daily life of families, it is not enough to alleviate a concern that is real. That concern does not disappear just because a graph points to a descending curve, which we celebrate; we have, logically, to do more, much more. That is why we have taken measures, such as the elimination of VAT on basic foodstuffs. And, look, according to a recent study by the European Commission, this measure is already being transferred to the consumer, and I hope it will be consolidated in the data we have on inflation developments. This report by the European Commission says that overall the reform has achieved its objective of reducing prices, at least immediately after its implementation. In addition to the elimination of VAT on basic foodstuffs and the reduction of VAT on other foodstuffs, the restriction on rent increases and the suspension of evictions of tenants without alternative housing is extended. In the field of transport, as you know, we have extended the reduction by 30% of regional or local competition transport allowances. I will never forget a student interview in a written media media saying that thanks to the gratuitousness in the vicinity she has been able to continue studying at the university. Obviously, this 30% premium for autonomous and local competition transport is conditional on the autonomous and local governments financing at least an additional 20%, so that this discount for the user reaches up to 50%. And we have added the 50% discount increase for multi-travel tickets and tickets in the Canary Islands and the Balearic Islands. With the new year, we maintain the gratuity, as you know, of rolling stock, proximity and mid-distance on state-owned bus lines and continue to increase the discount bonuses by 50% for recurring high-speed users. In total, ladies and gentlemen, in order to get an idea of the impact and social response that this measure has had, we are talking about almost 2.5 million fertilizers in 2022 and more than 1.3 million since the beginning of the year 2023. We are a few days away, a few weeks away, from having started the year 2023. I am aware, ladies and gentlemen, that we have to do much more. I am aware, for example, of the impact of the rise of euribor on the mortgage burden for thousands of households. For this reason, we have promoted a reduction in the rate applicable for five years of lack for families with incomes below EUR 25 200 per year. We have also put in place an additional protective measure to freeze the quota for a year where the relative effort is much higher. And here too, ladies and gentlemen, we have to learn lessons from the past, because we cannot leave the majority aside in exchange for protecting certainties from privileged minorities. Together with families, what we do is act with determination in the field of energy. I have referred to the effort that Spain has made to lead this reform of the electricity market at European level, a reform that translates into a price reduction, so that an essential good is not an unattainable luxury, so that no family has to choose between eating or turning on their radiator. And to the Iberian solution, to which I have already referred, I would also like to add the strengthening that we have made of the electrical social bond, the increase in the allocation of the thermal social bond, the establishment of the maximum price for butane candy, the reduction of VAT and many other taxes on electricity and gas, or the prohibition of the suspension of the supply of electricity, gas and water. Thirdly, of course, support our small and medium-sized enterprises, the strategic sectors and also industry. We do so with more lines of endorsement for self-employed people, for companies affected by the increase in energy costs and also for raw materials. We help particularly affected sectors - remember the land transport of goods, passengers, agriculture, livestock, fishing or ceramics - with ICO lines of liquidity, with direct aid and also with a decarbonisation PART, which will help to modernise this important industry. We have also extended the fuel rebate to carriers, farmers, shipping companies and fishermen. We have boosted specific direct aid for large electricity consumers and, as I said earlier, the intensive gas industry. Finally, we have made progress in what is called fiscal justice; that is, to fairly distribute burdens, costs and sacrifices in a context such as the current one, which I believe is not only economic efficiency, but also a very relevant moral imperative. It is the only way to strengthen the public, which is of everyone and everyone, which saves us when everything else fails. We have created some new tributes, and all of them have a common denominator that I would like to share with you, and it is to demand an additional effort from those who have and who have benefited most from the price escalation and the rise of the types. And I say this openly, ladies and gentlemen, because when we have to make an extra effort in difficult times like the present, that effort must fall on those who enjoy a much more relaxed position. That's what fiscal justice is about, and I think it's the most decent thing we can do in these times of uncertainty. In return, what are we doing? Well, in return we have not only put in place this €45 billion to protect people, but there is also a selective tax reform that includes a reduction in labour yields that will benefit one in two workers in our country. We also include a reduction in the taxation of self-employed workers, which will benefit almost a million of them. We also lower company tax for small and medium-sized enterprises that charge up to EUR 1 million and which will reach more than 400 thousand companies in our country. These and other additional measures involve unprecedented resource mobilization. We are talking - again, I repeat - about EUR 45 billion in public funds to protect people, businesses and industries, where it is most needed and at the most appropriate time. I recognize, ladies and gentlemen, that these are progressive, social measures, the same measures which are opening up in many other European countries, just the opposite ones, as I said at the beginning of my speech, which were applied during the financial crisis and which today, fortunately, rejects the majority of European countries and also the international consensus. I believe that we are all very aware not only of the lessons of ten years ago, but also of the failure of neoliberal recipes following the crisis in the United Kingdom, which led to the conservative government of Liz Truss. Ladies and gentlemen, as you know, last week I once again had the opportunity to participate in the World Economic Forum. During my stay I had to answer two questions repeatedly, which I would like to share with you: what were the recipes - I quote textually - of the Spanish economic success - literally said by the organizers - and whether I thought they could serve as an example for other countries. [[Applauds]] It is true - you said it - that we have to continue to walk in fiscal justice, but I will give you some data that I have here that seem relevant to me. We have made silent reforms that contribute to or advance that fiscal justice. For example, we have set the minimum rate of 15% for corporation tax; we have limited the exemptions; we have raised the rates applicable to very high labour incomes, more than EUR 300,000; also for capital, more than EUR 200 000; we have approved a tax on large fortunes complementary to the existing capital tax; and all this we have done together with the tax on large financial institutions, the tax on large energy companies, the whole debate we have opened in Europe on the so-called fallen profits of the sky, and we have also accompanied it with selective rebates to the middle class and to workers. There was a lot of talk here on the part of the opposition, Mr Rufián - you will agree with me - that we had to deflate the IRPF tariff, but what this government has actually done has been far more serious. What we have done has been to help middle classes with selective tax cuts. And I am not only referring to the VAT on electricity or gas, but we have reduced corporation tax to SMEs from 25 to 23 %, to those who charge less than EUR 1 million; we have changed the income tax on natural persons; we have raised the minimum tax on income from EUR 14 000 to EUR 15 000, and we have also extended the limit on reductions from EUR 18,000 to EUR 21,000; and for the self-employed what we have done has been to include an additional 5% reduction in the IRPF in the net performance of modules. ParlaMint-ES_2023-01-24-CD230124.u235	Thank you, Madam President. It is already ten and twenty o'clock at night and I would like to thank the majority of the parliamentary groups for their tone. It is true that in this Congress we hear things that sometimes far exceed parliamentary courtesy, but I am not going to get into it. Obviating that Spain is growing more than the European average and saying that Spain is worse than the rest of the European economies, it is simply a lie that is not contrasted, does not sympathize with the data of the Spanish economy. And that doesn't mean that things don't have to improve, it doesn't mean that we don't have a time of uncertainty ahead of us, of a lot of complexity arising from the crisis of this war in Ukraine. The same applies to employment and the same to inflation. But, ladies and gentlemen, since last 24 February - this is how I began my first intervention; today it is eleven months since the start of the Putin war in Ukraine - the Government of Spain has not hidden the gravity of the situation, nor has the Government of Spain told the Spaniards that what we had before us was going to be easy, but we have told the Spaniards that, both at European level and at national level, we were going to put all the resources of the State to defend their income, to protect their well-being and to make the transformations that allow Europe and Spain to be freer and more autonomous in the face of blackmails that can make autocracies, whether Russian or any other. In that context, ladies and gentlemen, I have tried to make this appearance. I would like to thank all the parliamentary groups that have made it possible to validate the sixth aid package in response to the war in Ukraine, because, indeed, that is what we are helping people with. And beyond the disqualifications and insults of those who have nothing more than that, that insult, because they have no project, I want to stay with the best of what has happened in the General Courts, and it is the demand with our vote and with our action for a useful policy, which is a policy that serves the citizenry and that is far from all this noise with which, basically, the only thing they try to hide is that this government, together with a broad parliamentary majority, what it does is to protect the middle class, the workers, and to do that transformation task - I also say with conviction, because I have heard it - that I can assure you, ladies and gentlemen, that it is admired and recognized outside Spain, in many countries of Europe with left-wing governments and also with conservative governments.
